Leonard chuckled. "I thought you'd grown accustomed to his habits. Yet you're still waiting innocently for him to remember everything...

"We should be wary if the day ever comes when the captain forgets nothing."

Would that indicate a loss of control? Klein nodded gravely. "Is the forgetfulness peculiar to him? I thought it resulted from the Sleepless pathway."

Staying awake through the night commonly damaged memory.

"More precisely, it's a symptom of Nightmare. When dreams and reality intertwine, distinguishing one from the other becomes difficult. They must remember which things never occurred in reality..."

Before Leonard could elaborate, they reached Iron Cross Street and found Corpse Collector Frye at the public-carriage stop.

Frye wore a round black hat and matching trench coat and carried a leather case. His face was so pale Klein wondered whether he might collapse at any moment. The coldness surrounding him caused everyone else at the stop to keep their distance.

After exchanging nods, the three passed Smyrin Bakery and turned onto Lower Iron Cross Street.

Noise immediately engulfed them. Vendors loudly advertised clam soup, fried fish, ginger beer, and fruit, forcing pedestrians to slow as they navigated the crowds.

It was already past five, and people were returning home. Children moved among them, watching coldly while focusing upon pockets and purses.

Having once lived nearby and often bought inexpensive cooked food here, Klein knew the area well. "Watch for thieves."

Leonard smiled. "Don't worry about them."

He tugged at his shirt and adjusted his holster, exposing the revolver.

Every interested gaze immediately turned away, and the crowd instinctively opened a path.

Klein stared briefly, then hurried after Leonard and Frye with his head lowered, hoping no acquaintance would recognize him. Benson and Melissa still knew people here; they had not moved far away.

The three left the busy peddlers behind and entered the true Lower Street.

Everyone there wore old, ragged clothing. They regarded well-dressed strangers with caution and with the greed of vultures watching a meal, waiting for weakness. Leonard's revolver prevented trouble.

"We'll begin with yesterday's death--Mrs. Lauwis, who assembled matchboxes for a living." Leonard consulted his notes and pointed nearby. "Number 134, first floor."

As they approached, thin children in tattered clothes abandoned their games and hid around corners, watching with fearful curiosity.

"Their arms and legs are as thin as matchsticks," Leonard sighed before entering the building.

A blend of odors assaulted Klein: urine, sweat, mold, and burning coal.

He instinctively pinched his nose and saw Bitsch Mountbatten waiting inside.

The officer with the yellow-brown mustache plainly envied Leonard's inspector rank.

"Sir, I asked Lauwis to wait in his room," Mountbatten said in his distinctive shrill voice.

He did not recognize the healthier, better-dressed Klein. Concerned only with currying favor, he led the three officers to the Lauwis family's first-floor room.

The apartment was painfully simple. An upright bunk bed occupied one side, while a table covered in glue and cardboard stood on the right. Matchbox frames filled one corner. An old cabinet on the left stored both clothing and utensils.

A stove, a chamber pot, and small supplies of coal and wood crowded the entrance. Two filthy mattresses covered the center of the floor. A man slept beneath a torn blanket, leaving almost no room to walk.

On the lower bunk lay a woman whose skin was already cold. She showed no sign of life.

Beside the corpse sat a man in his thirties with greasy hair, a haggard face, and lifeless eyes.

"Lauwis, these officers are here to examine the body and question you," Mountbatten shouted without regard for the sleeping tenant.

The dispirited man looked up. "Didn't someone already inspect her and ask me questions?"

His gray-blue worker's uniform had been patched many times.

"Answer when you're told! Why so many questions?" Mountbatten berated him, then turned to the Nighthawks. "Officers, this is Lauwis. The deceased is his wife. Our preliminary conclusion was sudden illness."

Klein and the others tiptoed toward the bunk.

The thin-lipped, high-nosed Frye silently patted Lauwis's shoulder, signaling him to move aside for the examination.

Klein pointed toward the sleeping man. "Who is he?"

"M-my tenant." Lauwis rubbed his forehead. "The room costs three soli and ten pence a week. I work at the harbor, and my wife earned two and a quarter pence for every crate of matchboxes she assembled. Each crate contains as many as a hundred and thirty boxes. We have a child too, so we must rent out the remaining space. We charge one soli a week for the mattress...

"Another tenant works at the theater and doesn't return before ten. He sold the daytime use of his mattress to this man, who guards the theater at night. He pays sixpence a week."

As Lauwis stammered through the explanation, Klein looked toward the crates.

One hundred and thirty matchboxes earned only two and a quarter pence--roughly the price of two pounds of black bread. How many could she complete in a day?[1]

Leonard surveyed the room. "Did your wife behave unusually before her death?"

Lauwis pointed toward the left side of his chest. "Beginning last week--perhaps the week before--she complained of pressure here and said she couldn't breathe."

Signs of heart disease? A natural death? Klein asked, "Did you witness her death?"

"She stopped working after sunset. Candles and gas cost more than matchboxes... She said she was exhausted and asked me to speak with the children while she rested. When I checked again, she had already stopped breathing."

His grief finally broke through.

Klein and Leonard asked several more questions but found nothing unnatural.

After exchanging a look, Leonard said, "Mr. Lauwis, please wait outside. We need to conduct a thorough examination, and you won't want to watch."

"All right." Lauwis rose anxiously.

Mountbatten kicked the tenant awake and drove him out, then closed the door and stood guard outside.

"Well?" Leonard asked Frye.

"A heart attack," Frye answered with certainty, withdrawing his hands.

Klein took out a halfpenny and considered an appropriate statement.

Mrs. Lauwis's heart attack resulted from supernatural causes? No, that's too narrow and might mislead me... "Supernatural factors influenced Mrs. Lauwis's death." Yes, use that.

Repeating the statement, he approached the body. His eyes darkened, and he tossed the coin.

It rang against the floor before landing in his palm.

The king's portrait faced upward.

Affirmative.

Supernatural factors had influenced Mrs. Lauwis's death.

[1] In the Victorian era, a crate held 144 matchboxes and earned 2.25 pence. A woman working all day could complete at most seven crates.

Chapter 120: The Workhouse

"Supernatural factors were involved..." Klein's eyes returned to normal as he looked at Leonard and Frye.

Leonard chuckled. "Very professional. Worthy of the title Seer."

Are you hinting at something? Klein wondered.

Frye opened his case and removed a silver knife and several other tools. After a pause, he said, "The corpse tells me she died of a sudden heart attack. Can you obtain a more detailed answer?"

Klein nodded gravely. "I can combine a mediumship ritual with dream divination. Hopefully, Mrs. Lauwis's lingering spirituality will reveal something."

Frye retreated two steps. "Try it."

He glanced sideways at Klein and sighed without altering his flat tone. "You're becoming accustomed to this sort of situation."

It's not as though I wanted to... Klein nearly wept. He took out his extracts, essential oils, and herbal powders and swiftly prepared the mediumship ritual.

Within the spiritual barrier, he invoked the Evernight Goddess's honorific names and recited his prayer in Hermes.

Wind soon circled him, and the light dimmed.

Klein's eyes turned entirely black. He repeated,

"The cause of Mrs. Lauwis's death.

"The cause of Mrs. Lauwis's death."

Standing upright, he entered a dream and saw a translucent spirit lingering near the corpse.

His illusory right hand reached out and touched Mrs. Lauwis's remaining spirituality.

Light burst before him, and scenes flashed past one after another.

A thin, sallow woman in ragged clothing assembled matchboxes without rest.

She abruptly stopped and clutched her chest.

She spoke with her two children.

Her body swayed as she struggled for breath.

While she bought black bread, someone patted her back.

Again and again, she suffered symptoms of heart disease.

Exhausted, she lay down and never awakened.

Klein scrutinized every detail for traces of supernatural influence. The visions ended without yielding a clear clue. As the blur shattered, he left the dream and returned to reality.

After dismissing the spiritual barrier, he addressed the waiting Frye and faintly amused Leonard.

"Nothing directly supernatural appeared. Most scenes confirmed that Mrs. Lauwis had suffered from heart disease for some time. Only one stood out: someone patted her on the back. The hand was pale and slender, apparently belonging to a woman."

"Families like this don't visit physicians unless the illness becomes unbearable," Leonard said with a poet's melancholy. "They can't afford to spend a day waiting at a charitable hospital. Missing one day's work might mean no food the next."

Frye looked at the corpse and sighed softly.

Leonard quickly emerged from his reflection. "Are you suggesting the supernatural influence occurred when the woman touched Mrs. Lauwis?"

"Yes, but that is only my interpretation," Klein cautioned. "Divination is always ambiguous."

He and Leonard moved to the other side of the bed, allowing Frye to conduct a more thorough examination undisturbed.

When finished, Frye cleaned and stored his tools, covered the body, and turned around.

"She died from natural heart disease. There is no doubt."

Leonard paced to the door and stood there for some time.

"That's enough for now. We'll investigate the West Borough workhouse and see whether another incident provides a connecting clue."

"All we can do is hope," Klein agreed, still puzzled.

Frye picked up his case and carefully stepped between the two floor mattresses without touching anyone's blanket.

Leonard opened the door first. "Mr. Lauwis, you and your tenant may return."

Klein added, "Don't bury the body yet. Wait another day in case a further examination is required."

"A-all right, officer." Lauwis bowed anxiously, then said with numb bewilderment, "Actually, I... I don't have enough money to bury her. I need several days to save it. Fortunately, the weather is growing colder."

Klein stared. "You intend to keep the body here for days?"

Lauwis forced a smile. "Yes. The colder weather helps. At night I can move her to the table. When we eat, I can carry her back to the bed..."

Frye interrupted in a perfectly calm voice. "I left burial money beside your wife."

He immediately left, paying no attention to Lauwis's shock and grateful cries.

Following him, Klein wondered what the family would have done had the weather remained as hot as June or July.

Choose a dark, windy night and throw her into the Tussock or Khoy River? Dig a hole somewhere and bury her secretly?

Laws requiring cemetery burial had existed for more than a thousand years, since the previous Epoch's end. The seven Churches and every royal house supported them to reduce water ghosts, zombies, and restless wraiths.

Governments provided land, while Churches guarded and patrolled it. Cremation and burial carried only minimal fees to cover necessary labor.

Yet the poorest still could not afford them.

After leaving 134 Lower Iron Cross Street, the three Nighthawks parted from Bitsch Mountbatten and silently turned toward a nearby workhouse.

As they approached, Klein saw a tremendous line, like the queues outside suddenly fashionable shops in the Foodaholic Empire.

"A hundred people--no, nearly two hundred," he murmured in surprise.

Those waiting wore tattered clothes and numb expressions, glancing impatiently toward the workhouse entrance only occasionally.

Frye slowed. "Every workhouse limits the number of homeless poor admitted each day. Places are assigned according to the queue, though applicants are examined and rejected if they fail the criteria."

"The recent recession has worsened matters," Leonard sighed.

"What happens to those who don't get in?" Klein asked.

"They can try another workhouse. Their hours differ, but every one has a similar queue. Some begin waiting at two in the afternoon." Frye paused. "Most others go hungry. Then they become too weak to seek work and enter a cycle leading directly to death. Those unable to endure eventually abandon the law..."

After several silent seconds, Klein sighed. "The newspapers never report this. Mr. Frye, I've rarely heard you speak so much."

"I was once a pastor at a workhouse belonging to the Goddess," Frye answered with his customary coldness.

At the entrance, they displayed their identification to the doorkeeper, who had been surveying the applicants arrogantly, and were admitted.

The workhouse occupied a converted church. Mattresses and hammocks filled the former worship hall. The pungent mixture of sweat and diseased feet permeated every corner.

Inside and outside, impoverished families labored. Some broke stones with hammers; others picked oakum. No one was idle.

"To prevent the poor from becoming dependent upon workhouses and turning into idlers, the Poor Law of 1336 limits each stay to five days," Frye explained emotionlessly. "Afterward, they are expelled. During those five days, they must perform prison labor such as breaking stone or picking oakum."

Leonard opened his mouth, his tone hovering between mockery and explanation. "Upon leaving this workhouse, they can try another--if they win a place. Heh. Some people evidently consider poverty a crime."

"What is picking oakum?" Klein could think of nothing else to ask.

"Unraveling fibers from old rope. They're useful for sealing gaps in ships." Frye stopped beside a blackened patch on the floor.

Several minutes later, the workhouse director and pastor hurried over. Both were in their forties.

Pointing at the burned mark, Leonard asked, "Salus started the fire here and killed only himself?"

The broad, uneven forehead of the director caught the light. He examined the spot with blue eyes and nodded.

"Yes."

"Did Salus behave strangely beforehand?" Klein asked.

The director considered. "According to the man who slept beside him, Salus repeatedly said things such as, 'The Lord has abandoned me,' 'The world is filthy,' and 'I have nothing left.' He was resentful and hopeless. But no one expected him to smash the kerosene lamps and try to burn down the building while everyone slept. Thank the Lord, someone discovered him in time and prevented the atrocity."

Klein and Leonard questioned those who had slept nearby and the guard who stopped the fire, but learned nothing new.

Spirit Vision, divination, and other methods confirmed that no one was lying or deliberately misleading them.

"Salus had evidently contemplated revenge and self-destruction for a long time. The case seems entirely ordinary," Leonard concluded once the director and pastor had left.

"My divination found no supernatural influence," Klein agreed.

"Then we'll tentatively eliminate the Salus fire."

Frye suddenly spoke. "No. Another possibility remains. Someone might have instigated him without using any directly supernatural method."

Klein's eyes brightened. "Yes--like the Instigator we encountered before!"

Instigator Tris!

But that wouldn't explain Mrs. Lauwis's death... Klein frowned slightly.

Chapter 121: Leonard's Hypothesis

After hearing Klein and Frye's theories, Leonard tugged at his collar and paced.

"Then we must investigate everyone in the workhouse who contacted Salus, along with everyone he met after bankruptcy drove him from his home. Very troublesome... And time is critical. Let's divide the questioning here, then investigate the third reported West Borough death. The police can handle the remainder."

"All right," Klein agreed immediately.

Frye offered no objection and turned toward the men who had slept near Salus.

Klein was about to question someone when Leonard caught his eye and gestured with his chin toward the workhouse's side hall.

What does he want? Pretending to notice nothing, Klein wandered through the main hall and followed Leonard into the side chamber while Frye was occupied. They passed a partition and entered a deserted corner.

"I have a hypothesis," Leonard said, stopping before a broken window.

Klein looked around in confusion. "What hypothesis?"

Leonard's deep-green eyes fixed upon him. "Without supernatural interference, what do you think would have happened to Mrs. Lauwis?"

After considering, Klein answered gravely, "The same thing, only a week or two later--perhaps a month. A family like hers would consult a physician only when she could endure no more. Once her heart disease worsened, nothing would save her."

"And Salus? Without someone instigating him, how would his life have ended?"

"The reports describe a man furious about his bankruptcy and enraged that no one rescued him. Sooner or later, he probably would have sought revenge--though against the employer who ruined him or the bank employees who seized his home, not against the workhouse residents."

"And what would that revenge have produced?" Leonard pressed.

"He had plainly decided to end his life. Whatever the result, he would have died," Klein answered with certainty.

Leonard nodded and revealed his familiar frivolous smile.

"Then can we conclude that both Mrs. Lauwis and Salus were destined to die soon?"

As an experienced keyboard warrior, Klein immediately formed a guess.

"You mean some supernatural force advanced their deaths? But why?"

"More precisely, it shortened--stole--their life force. Life force is an excellent material for summoning evil gods or devils and for casting terrifying curses," Leonard corrected.

"Summoning evil gods or devils, or casting curses..." Klein studied Leonard's emerald eyes. "You sound remarkably certain, but our sample contains only two cases."

Leonard laughed cynically. "Klein, we needn't pretend with each other. I watched you escape Sealed Artifact 2-049's control. I know you're unusual. And surely you've sensed that I'm not an ordinary Beyonder either."

His smile disappeared.

"I've told you before: many special people in this world accomplish things others cannot. People like you... and me.

"This world has a long history and contains many extraordinary objects that people seek to possess and control. Such people want to become the stars of their own stories. They are rare, but there cannot be only one or two.

"I don't believe a Beyonder becomes wicked merely by possessing secrets. We needn't know the source or meaning of every unusual ability. So long as you don't endanger me, the Nighthawks, or Tingen, you remain my companion.

"I hope you'll treat me the same way. Naturally, we shouldn't tell our superiors. Those old men are conservative and assume special people like us will inevitably lose control or succumb to an evil god's temptation."

I possess more secrets than you could possibly imagine... Klein answered candidly, "I agree. I'll judge your conduct and motives, not your unusual nature, and I'll try not to pry into your secrets."

He added inwardly, Actually, I care deeply and am tremendously curious. I'm only restraining myself. What encounter did Leonard have? What extraordinary object does he possess?

Leonard loosened his shirt collar and chuckled. "I'm pleased we understand each other.

"In an adventure novel, this would be the meeting of two protagonists--the moment the wheels of history begin turning."

How shameless. Klein offered a perfunctory smile. He knew Emperor Roselle had coined the phrase about history's wheels.

Leonard paced rapidly, his green eyes brightening.

"Very well. I'll be honest. I'm confident that these victims would all have died within three months, but someone used a particular method to advance their deaths into the past two weeks. The culprit probably intends to summon an evil god or devil, or cast a large and terrible curse."

"Choosing victims already near death would conceal the murders," Klein reasoned. "The police would see nothing suspicious, and the Nighthawks, Mandated Punishers, and Machinery Hivemind wouldn't interfere during the preparations."

"Exactly. Three healthy people suddenly dropping dead would immediately trigger an investigation," Leonard agreed.

"Then how do we find the ritual altar? Whether summoning something or preparing a curse, the culprit needs an altar. The stolen life force must also be stored somewhere similar." Lacking other clues, Klein chose to trust Leonard's theory.

There is no harm in trying.

Leonard laughed. "Isn't that your specialty? Imagine the surroundings of such an altar.

"A dense aura of death centered upon it. No living creature except the ritualist within ten meters. A temperature at least five degrees below the surrounding area, with a constant cold wind. Mrs. Lauwis's stolen vitality and that of the others sealed inside a wall of spirituality..."

He teasingly added, "Surely you can divine the approximate location of an altar with those characteristics."

Klein frowned. "If it's within Tingen, perhaps. I need a silent place without interruptions--my home, for example--and personal belongings from Mrs. Lauwis and the other victims."

His heart skipped. Leonard knew rather too much about dark rituals.

"No problem." Leonard laughed, stepped around him, and returned to the hall without another word.

What a peculiar man... Klein silently cursed and followed.

When Leonard found Frye taking notes, he adopted a solemn tone. "I have a theory I want Klein to test."

"What theory?" Frye asked coldly.

"I'll tell you if it produces results. I don't want Rozanne and the others laughing at me." Leonard offered the whimsical excuse and changed the subject.

Frye did not press him. Following their instructions, he obtained personal possessions belonging to Salus and Mrs. Lauwis from the nearby police station, then met his companions at Klein's home.

"Wait in the sitting room and don't allow anyone to disturb me." Klein checked his pocket watch.

It was nearly six. Melissa could return at any moment.

"Trust us," Leonard replied, hands on his hips as he paced. Frye sat silently upon the sofa.

Does Leonard have attention deficit disorder? Klein pursed his lips and went upstairs. He locked his bedroom and sealed it with a wall of spirituality.

He established an altar and prayed for the Goddess's aid in excluding interference.

Then he wrote a deliberately broad divination statement:

"The location of the altar."

Holding the paper and the dead victims' belongings, Klein lay upon his bed. He recalled Leonard's description and silently repeated the statement seven times.

He did not ascend above the gray fog. The mysterious Leonard waited downstairs and might notice something unusual. Besides, with the Seer potion nearly digested, assistance from the ritual should suffice.

If it failed, Klein could later seek an opportunity to try above the fog. Summoning an evil god or devil threatened Benson, Melissa, and himself.

With Cogitation's aid, he swiftly entered a dream and saw a hazy, fragmented world.

An image emerged.

A two-story gray-blue house stood beneath the sunset. Every ground-floor window was tightly shut, and dark curtains left no gaps, though they expanded and contracted from time to time.

The surrounding soil was dark brown and utterly barren. Shadows seemed to cover the ruined, gloomy garden.

Nearby, a river flowed in silence.

After a time, Klein left the dream without seeing anything further.

Leonard's hypothesis was correct... But where is that house? Tingen has countless waterways--West Borough, Southwest Borough, the harbor, the university district...

Klein opened his eyes and rubbed his temples, his expression grave.

Chapter 122: The Target Building

Inside the sitting room at 2 Daffodil Street, burnished by the colors of dusk, Klein stood before the oriel window and addressed Frye and Leonard.

"My divination produced an image. I saw a gray-blue two-story house. Every ground-floor window was tightly shut behind drawn curtains. Several yards of barren brown soil surrounded it, without flowers or greenery. The garden was ruined and oppressively gloomy--the sort of place found in a horror story.

"The only useful landmark is a fairly broad river nearby.

"It may be the Tussock or the Khoy. We'll have to eliminate possibilities one by one. Hopefully, we still have time."

The Tussock was Loen's largest river. It descended from the Mirminsk Mountains in the northwest, flowed southeast through Midseashire and Awwa County, passed the capital Backlund, and emptied into the sea near Pritz Harbor.

Within Tingen, it crossed the southwestern corner of West Borough and the harbor in South Borough. The Khoy originated in the northern York Mountains, passed through the university district in East Borough, and joined the Tussock locally.

They were Tingen's two principal rivers. The remaining waterways were merely streams, none wide enough to match the dream.

The pale, aloof Frye nodded. With no other clues, elimination was the only practical method.

Leonard smiled. "Perhaps we can narrow the search further."

"How?" Klein frowned and checked his silver, vine-patterned pocket watch.

Leonard chuckled. "A criminal acting according to a plan will instinctively select victims far from the altar to protect himself.

"Only after exhausting the supply of people near death in distant areas would he target those nearby.

"Let's examine the reports again and exclude districts where deaths rose sharply above the average."

Klein's eyes brightened. "An excellent conjecture!"

At the same time, he sighed inwardly. I truly possess no talent for detective work.

Frye picked up the documents from the coffee table and reread them.

Several minutes later, his hoarse voice deepened. "One district fits. Only one."

"Which?" Klein asked.

Frye passed the papers to Leonard and pursed his thin lips. "West Borough."

West Borough? Klein clenched a fist. "Then we'll search the southwestern section. It isn't very large."

"Agreed." Leonard waved the papers as though narrowing the area had not been his own suggestion.

A two-wheeled carriage rolled slowly along the muddy road. Beside it, the sunset's red-orange light shimmered across a broad river.

Klein and Frye watched from opposite windows, examining each house for gray-blue walls, a dilapidated garden, and, if visible, closed ground-floor curtains.

Leonard lounged against the carriage wall and hummed a popular local melody.

The twilight scenery drifted past. At the edge of his vision, Klein suddenly caught sight of a gray-blue two-story building.

A gloomy, ruined garden lay before it.

"There!" Klein said in a suppressed voice.

Before he finished, Frye and Leonard crowded beside him until scarcely any space remained.

As the carriage drew closer, they saw dark curtains covering every ground-floor window.

Klein needed no further divination. He was certain this was the house from his dream--the location of the evil altar.

They did not stop. The driver continued beyond the target as though they were ordinary passersby.

Once a turn concealed the house, Leonard ordered the carriage to halt.

"Klein, take the carriage back to Zouteland Street and bring the captain," Leonard said, snapping his fingers and smiling.

He sees me as a novice who shouldn't participate in such a dangerous operation. This fellow is actually considerate... Klein realized.

Frye nodded. "You only recently began combat training. Your role is support."

I understand. Anyone who killed so many people for a ritual won't be an easy opponent. Only the captain can make this less terrifying... Klein drew a breath and accepted the rational decision.

He looked from Leonard to Frye and forced a smile. "Be careful."

"Don't worry. I value my life. Until the captain arrives, we'll only observe from a distance," Leonard promised.

Frye said nothing and merely picked up his case.

After a moment's silence, Klein produced a copper penny. "Let me divine your prospects first."

His eyes darkened as he repeated, "What happens here will produce a favorable outcome," and tossed the coin.

Ting!

It spun through the air and landed securely in his palm with the king's portrait facing upward.

Klein exhaled in relief.

"The symbol is vague and permits other interpretations. Remaining cautious is still the most important thing," he warned in the manner of a Seer.

Leonard had already turned away. He waved and jumped down.

"You nag as much as my eighty-year-old grandmother."

Frye nodded solemnly and followed with his case.

Watching his companions head back toward the target, Klein touched the revolver beneath his arm and told the driver, "Zouteland Street."

Hired by the hour, the driver raised no objection and urged the horses onward.

At 36 Zouteland Street, Rozanne, Mrs. Orianna, and the others had already left for the evening. Blackthorn Security Company was unusually dim and silent.

Dunn sat upon a guest sofa with the gas lamps extinguished. In his black trench coat, he seemed to merge with the darkness.

"Did you find anything?" His deep voice startled Klein, who had been searching for him.

Klein turned quickly toward Dunn's gray eyes. "Yes. We..."

He swiftly explained Leonard's bold theory, the divination that confirmed it, and their discovery of the house.

Leonard's private confidence and unusual nature were plainly irrelevant and not worth mentioning.

Dunn interrupted occasionally. When the report ended, he rose abruptly and headed for the door.

Partway down the stairs, he turned. "I nearly forgot. Stay here in case an emergency occurs."

"All right." Klein nodded solemnly.

Apart from Kenley guarding Chanis Gate, every other Nighthawk was already in the field.

Dunn descended several more steps, then stopped again. Putting on his hat, he called through the doorway,

"Lock the door and come with me. You won't participate in the fighting. First, you should experience the atmosphere of an operation. Second, we may need ritualistic magic during the final search or examination.

"Until everything is over, remain at least fifty meters away. Do not approach the building!"

After a stunned moment, Klein nodded firmly. "Understood!"

The sun sank beneath the horizon, turning the surging Tussock River dark and ominous.

Clouds concealed the crimson moon. The gray-blue two-story house resembled a monster lurking in shadow.

Its garden was perfectly still, without insects or any other sign of life.

Watching from a distance, Klein's palms sweated and his body trembled.

He felt as though countless horrors hid within, waiting hungrily for a bloody feast.

Dunn, Leonard, and Frye advanced cautiously toward the house and disappeared into the darkness.

Upstairs, in an unlit bedroom, a sweet, round-faced young woman sat before her dressing table and carefully examined the results of an elaborate skincare routine.

A silver mirror lay near her right hand. Its surface was so crudely polished that it could barely reflect an image.

Suddenly, blood seeped from the mirror.

The gentle-looking Trissy's expression turned grave. She rose, approached the window, and silently looked outside.

Chapter 123: Beyonder Battle

Vines sprawled across the dilapidated garden beyond the windows. The river flowed quietly beneath the starlight, while a warm glow spilled from the nearby buildings.

Everything was silent, as though the night itself were holding its breath.

Trissy withdrew her gaze. Her features were ordinary when considered separately, yet together they gave her a striking beauty. She strode to the clothes rack and took down a long black hooded robe.

She swiftly put it on, fastening the buttons and belt before pulling the hood over her head. In an instant, she had transformed into an Assassin.

Trissy raised her right hand and brushed it across her face. The features beneath her hood immediately blurred.

Then she reached into a concealed pouch at her waist, drew out a handful of shimmering powder, and scattered it over herself while murmuring an incantation.

Her figure faded little by little, her outline disappearing as though erased from a page.

Once the concealment spell was complete, she slipped silently out of the bedroom. She crossed to the room opposite and opened its unbarred window.

With a light hop, Trissy landed on the windowsill and surveyed the grassy ground behind the building. Looking down toward the steel fence that seemed to blend into the darkness, she spotted Corpse Collector Frye climbing over it without a sound.

She took a deep breath and floated down like a feather, landing noiselessly on the grass.

Dressed in a black trench coat, Frye cautiously examined his surroundings with his custom revolver in hand, searching for any wraiths or evil spirits that might emerge.

He could see such entities directly.

Trissy crept up behind him. At some point, a dagger coated in a black substance had appeared in her hand.

Thrust!

She struck with lightning speed, driving the dagger into Frye's lower back.

But at that very moment, the scene before her shattered like an illusion.

Trissy discovered that she was still standing on the windowsill, still staring at the grassy field and steel fence.

Only now, Corpse Collector Frye was not alone outside the fence. Leonard Mitchell was there as well, aiming directly at the windowsill. Beside him stood Dunn Smith, captain of the Nighthawks. He leaned forward with his eyes closed and one hand pressed to his glabella as invisible ripples spread outward from him.

Trissy's pupils contracted. Everything she had just experienced had been a dream. She had fallen asleep without even realizing it!

Bang! Bang! Bang!

Leonard and Frye fired three shots, accurately striking the invisible target before she had fully shaken off the dream.

Crack!

Trissy's figure emerged, cracked, and then shattered completely into fragments of a crudely polished silver mirror.

Inside the building, the real Trissy turned and fled after completing her substitution spell. She raced down the corridor and stairs, sprinting all the way to the first floor.

Whoosh!

A sinister, freezing wind swept through the first floor. Invisible, translucent figures wandered dazedly through every corner of the building.

With her concealment broken, Trissy felt her body grow colder each time she passed through one of the spirits. By the time she reached the sacrificial altar, she could no longer suppress her shivering.

The altar consisted of a round table with a bone figurine at its center.

The figurine was roughly the size of an adult's head. Its eyes were only faintly delineated, but its form was unmistakably that of a beautiful woman.

Her hair stretched all the way from her head to her heels. Every strand was thick and distinct, resembling a venomous snake or writhing tentacle.

At the tip of each strand was a single eye. Some were open; others were closed.

Numerous crudely made puppets lay scattered around the figurine. Each bore a name and other identifying information--Joyce Mayer, for example.

Three candles stood on the table, their yellow-green flames flickering despite the cold, sinister wind.

Trissy bowed to the figurine and rapidly recited an incantation.

She swept the puppets aside and extinguished the candles, then picked up the figurine.

Whoosh!

The wind abruptly intensified, rattling the closed windows with savage force.

Clatter! Crash! Shards of glass flew in every direction.

Frye, who had just reached the far side of the building, did not dare storm recklessly into the room containing the altar. He shuddered as the cold seeped into his blood and bones, visibly slowing his movements.

Suddenly, something tightened around his ankles, as though invisible hands had seized them.

An unnatural chill spread upward from the point of contact. An ordinary Sequence 9 Beyonder would already have gone completely numb. But as a Corpse Collector, Frye was well accustomed to such situations.

He angled his revolver toward his feet and pulled the trigger, as if he could see both the identity and exact position of his attacker.

Bang!

A silver demon-hunting bullet tore through the air, drawing a piercing shriek.

The invisible figure dispersed, and Frye regained his mobility.

Elsewhere, Dunn Smith had tried to reach the second floor rather than launch a frontal assault on the altar. He, too, was caught in the freezing wind. His body stiffened just outside a shattered window.

Whoosh!

The curtain behind the window suddenly surged forward and engulfed Dunn, like a monster opening its jaws to swallow its prey.

The cloth wrapped tightly around his head as if it had come alive. The outline of Dunn's features pressed against its constricting surface.

On the verge of suffocation, Dunn stamped both feet down, straightened his knees, and twisted at the waist, loosening the curtain through sheer strength.

With his left hand, he seized one corner of the cloth around his head, ripped it away, and flung it to the ground.

Bang!

He fired at the remaining half of the curtain behind the window, stopping it before it could attack him again.

The curtain instantly went limp as dark red liquid oozed from it.

Whoosh!

Out in the garden, Leonard Mitchell was reciting a poem when the sinister wind struck him, carrying with it an overwhelming aura of death. His teeth began to chatter, making it difficult to pronounce the words.

The tangled weeds suddenly lengthened and coiled around his ankles. At the same time, a black shadow hurtled toward him on the violent wind.

Leonard's body had grown rigid. Unable to fire in time, he could only pull back his shoulder and raise his arm.

Thud!

The black shadow slammed into his forearm, its thorns piercing his skin.

It was a vivid, beautiful red flower of unknown origin.

Grimacing in pain, Leonard tore the bloodstained flower away and tossed it aside.

Bang!

He fired at the encroaching vines, and dark red liquid seeped from them.

Tap! Tap! Tap!

Leonard broke into a run, charging toward the shattered first-floor window behind which the altar stood.

The vines abruptly withdrew from the place where he had been standing, as though recoiling from something invisible.

Trissy had taken advantage of the chaos caused by the altar's destruction and the suspended ritual to conceal herself once more. She deceived the Nighthawks' Spirit Vision, slipped out of their encirclement, and maneuvered behind all three of them.

She extended her right hand. A cold gust immediately swept the flower stained with Leonard's blood into her palm.

Without slowing, Trissy clutched the flower, vaulted nimbly over the steel fence, and fled toward the Tussock River.

Leonard, who had just entered the first floor, abruptly turned his head as though listening to something.

His expression changed. He hurriedly pulled up his sleeve and examined the wound left by the flower.

Thanks to his constitution, the bleeding had already stopped. Only a patch of red swelling remained.

Leonard's face turned grim. He gripped his left index finger and tore the nail straight out!

Pain twisted his features, but he did not pause. Silently reciting something, he used the nail to reopen the clotted wound. Once it was coated in his dark red blood, he plucked several strands of hair from his scalp and wrapped them around it.

Beside the Tussock River, Trissy slowed and looked down at the flower in her hand.

She murmured an incantation, and a ball of illusory black flame sprang to life in her palm.

The flames engulfed the flower and reduced it to ash.

With that done, Trissy leaped into the river and disappeared beneath its surface.

At the same moment, Leonard tossed the bloodstained fingernail wrapped in his hair into a corner. It burst into flame, releasing a foul stench.

The nail and hair quickly vanished, leaving behind only a small pile of dust.

Leonard breathed a sigh of relief. He climbed through the window onto the first floor and spoke to Dunn and Frye, who were dismantling the altar.

"The target escaped, but that's all right. Our primary objective was to stop the ritual."

Dunn sighed and looked at the puppets scattered across the table.

"She was cautious and extremely powerful. She sensed our approach in advance. Otherwise... At the very least, she must be a Sequence 7 Beyonder.

"Signal Klein. Tell him to come over."

From their brief encounter within the dream, Dunn had determined that the enemy was a woman.

Chapter 124: Wrapping Up

Klein hid in the shadow of a building dozens of meters from the target. Faint gunshots reached his ears, followed by the howl of a violent wind.

If the enemy runs this way, should I draw my gun or pretend I didn't see her? he wondered, breaking out in a cold sweat.

A Beyonder capable of cutting short other people's lives through various means could not possibly be a mere Sequence 9 or Sequence 8. She was certainly not someone a Seer like him could confront head-on. Even if he sacrificed himself, he might not delay her long enough for Dunn and Leonard to catch up.

Fortunately, the Evernight Goddess--the Empress of Misfortune--seemed to have heard the prayers of her "loyal" guardian. No one came running toward Klein's hiding place.

A few minutes later, a melodious song drifted from the target building.

Klein cocked his head and listened carefully. It was the popular local tune Leonard Mitchell often hummed, complete with its vulgar lyrics.

Phew. He exhaled in relief, then stepped out of the shadows and headed toward the building, revolver in one hand and cane in the other.

That tune was the rendezvous signal he had agreed upon with Dunn and the others.

After taking two steps, however, Klein abruptly stopped. He propped his cane against the metal fence and transferred the revolver to his other hand.

Then he drew the silver chain from inside his sleeve and allowed the topaz pendant to hang freely.

Once the pendant had stopped swaying, Klein closed his eyes, entered Cogitation, and recited the divination statement:

"The singing just now was an illusion.

"The singing just now was an illusion."

After repeating it seven times, he opened his eyes and saw the pendant rotating counterclockwise.

"It wasn't an illusion..."

Klein put away the spirit pendulum, retrieved his cane, and quickly approached the arched metal gate leading to the target building. He shifted the black cane to his right hand, holding it alongside the revolver.

He reached out to push open the gate, only to be struck by a piercing chill. It felt as though someone had suddenly poured a bucket of ice water down the back of his neck.

Klein hissed through clenched teeth and jerked his hands away.

"It's like winter in here..."

By the dim starlight and the glow of a distant streetlamp, he peered through the fence into the garden. Withered branches and fallen flowers littered the brown earth, while a layer of white frost coated the leaves.

Incredible... Klein marveled inwardly. He curled his fingers and tapped his glabella, activating his Spirit Vision.

Returning the silver-inlaid cane to his left hand, he used it to push open the gate.

The hinges creaked as he slipped through sideways and stepped onto the stone path leading straight to the gray-blue building. On either side, twisted plants loomed in the darkness like ghouls.

The sight reminded Klein of all sorts of ghost stories and horror films.

He unconsciously held his breath and quickened his pace. But after only a few steps, something suddenly tapped him on the left shoulder.

Thump! Thump!

Klein's heart skipped a beat, then began hammering wildly.

He raised his revolver and slowly turned around.

In the dim light, he saw a slender branch hanging by a thread.

"So this is what they mean by scaring yourself..."

The corner of Klein's mouth twitched. He swung his cane and knocked the branch away.

As he continued forward, faint sobs sounded in his ears and hazy, translucent shadows emerged before his eyes.

Drawn by the breath of a living person and the warmth of flesh and blood, the shadows swarmed toward him.

Startled, Klein hurried through the entrance of the gray-blue building.

Is this what the Captain meant by "experiencing the atmosphere"? This is much more frightening than when I helped Sir Deville... That resentful spirit was more rigid than these shadows. At least she didn't actively attack me back then...

With those thoughts racing through his mind, Klein approached the altar in the center of the living room. It was a round table covered with crudely crafted puppets, among which stood three extinguished candles.

Dunn Smith stood before the altar with his back to Klein, picking up the puppets one by one and examining them.

Corpse Collector Frye watched the drifting shadows and reached out as if to comfort them, but his hand passed helplessly through their bodies. The shadows did not attack him, seemingly regarding him as one of their own.

Upon noticing Klein's arrival, Leonard Mitchell softened his voice into a gentle, captivating cadence.

"Calm is the morn without a sound,

"Calm as to suit a calmer grief,

"And only through the faded leaf

"The chestnut pattering to the ground." [1]

As Leonard recited the soothing lines, Klein seemed to behold a tranquil lake reflecting the moonlight, with a crimson moon hanging silently high above.

The restless shadows gradually calmed and stopped pursuing the warmth of the living Nighthawks among them.

Dunn set down the puppet in his hand, turned to Klein, and said, "This altar was prepared for a terrifying curse ritual. Fortunately, we've already destroyed it.

"First perform a ritual to soothe the remaining spirits. Afterward, try communicating with the dead and see whether you can obtain any clues."

Realizing he was no longer merely a burden, Klein straightened proudly.

"Yes, Captain."

He crossed to the altar in a few strides and reached out to sweep the puppets from the round table.

Out of the corner of his eye, he noticed that every puppet bore a name and the corresponding person's information.

"Captain, did you find anyone we know?" Klein asked casually.

Then he glanced at Dunn, just as Dunn looked back at him. Both fell silent.

I'm such an idiot... Why would I ask a question that tests the Captain's memory? Klein nearly covered his face and sighed.

Any other superior would surely find a chance to make my life miserable after that. Luckily, the Captain will probably forget... I wonder whether that's a strength or a weakness? he thought, half amused and half relieved.

After a brief silence, Dunn finally seemed to distinguish reality from the dream world.

"There is someone you know."

"Who?" Klein froze with one hand still extended, about to return a candle to its proper position.

"Joyce Mayer, the survivor of the Alfalfa incident," Dunn replied simply.

Joyce Mayer? Anna's fiance... Klein suddenly recalled Salus at the workhouse. Someone appeared to have instigated and misled him, inflaming his rage until he committed arson.

Klein withdrew his right hand and said gravely, "Instigator Tris?"

"Did he intend to sacrifice those whose lives had been cut short and curse every survivor of the Alfalfa incident? Was it because he didn't know who exposed his involvement and reported him to the police...?"

If Tris had pursued revenge directly, he could never have eliminated targets scattered throughout Tingen. After two or three murders, the Nighthawks, Mandated Punishers, or Machinery Hivemind would have noticed him, depriving him of any chance to continue his killing spree. Klein mentally filled in the reasoning behind Tris's actions.

Dunn nodded, then shook his head.

"Not every survivor--only those in Tingen. The curse ritual could affect people only within this area.

"Furthermore, the person conducting the ritual was a woman, not Tris."

Klein frowned. "Could she be an expert sent by the Theosophy Order to assist Tris?

"Yes, the Theosophy Order may have ties to the Demoness Sect. It wouldn't be unusual for one of their experts to be a woman."

Dunn smiled and replied in his deep voice, "I agree with your assessment. Although we encountered only that woman and not Tris, we can make several deductions. Perhaps she and Tris don't live together, or perhaps Tris had gone out to search for people nearing death."

Klein said nothing more. He arranged the three candles, took out the Full Moon Essence Oil, crimson sandalwood, and other ingredients, then swiftly prepared the altar.

After using a silver dagger to create a sealed spiritual wall, he began praying to the Evernight Goddess, the Mistress of Repose and Silence. He asked that the shadows inside and outside the building be completely soothed.

Unfortunately, when he subsequently tried to communicate with the dead, Klein caught only brief glimpses of what the spirits had witnessed before their deaths. He obtained no useful clues.

After settling the shadows into peaceful slumber beneath the dark night, he concluded the ritual and removed the spiritual wall. Then he shook his head and told the others, "The backlash from the disrupted ritual caused severe damage. The residual images of the person conducting it have been lost."

Dunn did not appear surprised. Pointing toward the stairs, he said, "Let's search the second floor and try again."

"All right."

Klein, Leonard, and Frye nodded in agreement.

The four Nighthawks ascended to the second floor and split up to search the rooms.

Eventually, they regrouped in a bedroom filled with a faint fragrance. Dresses lay scattered about, and several boxes stood open.

Dunn picked up a box from the dressing table and sniffed it.

"Are these cosmetics?"

"To be precise, they're skin-care products. Since Emperor Roselle's time, we no longer lump everything together under one broad term," Leonard explained with a smile. "Captain, there are certain things every gentleman ought to know."

Klein did not join their conversation. His gaze had settled on the mirror atop the dressing table.

A prominent crack ran across its surface, and shards of glass lay on the rug below.

"The Beyonder left in a hurry and didn't destroy it completely..." he said suddenly, his voice low. "I might be able to try something."

"I'll leave it to you," Dunn replied with confidence.

Klein quickly brought the candles upstairs and lit them before the broken mirror.

In the dim, flickering candlelight, he took out the Full Moon Essence Oil and the other necessary materials, then formed a spiritual wall.

Once everything was ready, he stood before the mirror, which reflected all three candle flames, and chanted in Hermes:

"I pray for the power of the dark night.

"I pray for the power of mystery.

"I pray for the Goddess's loving grace.

"I pray that the mirror may be briefly restored. I pray that it will reveal every person it has reflected within the past month."

As he recited the incantation, a fierce wind suddenly howled within the spiritual wall.

The fragments of glass rose from the floor, spun through the air, and returned to their original positions.

A gloomy radiance rippled across the cracked mirror. Klein swept a hand over its surface, and a figure suddenly appeared within the frame.

It was not Klein.

It was a gentle, sweet-looking young woman with a round face. Perhaps because the mirror had been shattered, or because the backlash from the disrupted ritual had affected the second floor as well, her features remained blurred and her true appearance could not be clearly distinguished.

Even so, Klein found her strangely familiar.

[1] Adapted from Alfred Tennyson's "In Memoriam A. H. H. OBIIT MDCCCXXXIII: 11."

Chapter 125: A Bold Idea

When confronted with an inexplicable sense of familiarity, most Sequence 9 Beyonders might struggle to search their memories, or simply dismiss the feeling altogether. A Seer, however, had other options.

Klein immediately ended the ritual and dispelled the spiritual wall. Taking out a sheet of paper, he wrote a divination statement:

"The source of this sense of familiarity."

Holding the paper, he sat on the edge of the bed and silently repeated the statement.

After the seventh repetition, his pupils darkened. With the aid of Cogitation, he drifted into sleep and began communing with his own spirituality.

Within the hazy, distorted dream world, Klein saw a carriage. Beside it stood a young woman in a long gray dress.

She had smooth black hair and a slightly rounded face. Her manner was gentle and pleasant, but her body trembled unnaturally.

The scene flickered. Klein saw the same pretty young woman again, this time crouching in the underground market as she spoke with someone.

The dream quickly receded, and Klein awoke knowing why the figure in the mirror had seemed so familiar.

He had met her before!

The first time was on Daffodil Street, near the Iron Cross Street district. That was the night the Captain and the others were pursuing Instigator Tris... There must be a connection.

After thinking for a few seconds, Klein prepared the ritual once more and asked for the Goddess's help in drawing a portrait of the enemy from his memories.

Dunn and the others waited in silence, careful not to disturb him. Only after Klein finished the sketch did they gather around to examine it.

"You've met her before?" Dunn asked.

Klein nodded slightly. "Yes. I saw her at a public carriage stop on Daffodil Street the night you were pursuing the Instigator. It was in the district near Iron Cross Street."

"Then there's a good chance she was the enemy we just encountered--the Instigator's accomplice." Dunn nodded thoughtfully.

Leonard suddenly interjected, "Doesn't this portrait look familiar to any of you? She looks remarkably like Instigator Tris!"

Klein froze, then turned back to the portrait and studied it carefully.

"You're right. They do look very similar. A round face, narrow eyes, a gentle air..."

The longer he stared, the more convincing Leonard's observation became. The main difference was that Instigator Tris had possessed rather ordinary features, whereas this young woman could genuinely be called pretty.

Klein looked up at Leonard and noticed him waggling his eyebrows as if trying to convey something.

What is he getting at? Klein wondered in confusion.

Dunn Smith speculated, "She might be the Instigator's sister. Perhaps she followed her brother into the Theosophy Order or the Demoness Sect."

Realizing how hopeless Klein was at interpreting his hint, Leonard sighed.

"I have a bold idea," he said seriously.

"What is it?" Dunn asked.

Leonard gave a succinct reply. "I believe this woman is Instigator Tris."

"What?" Frye exclaimed in astonishment.

Dunn frowned. "Are you saying Instigator Tris was actually a woman, or that he was a man disguised as one? No. From the dream, I can confirm that our enemy is female."

Having encountered plenty of imaginative and absurd stories in his previous life, Klein looked at the portrait again and immediately arrived at another possibility.

"Could Instigator Tris have turned into a woman?"

That would explain a great deal. Why had Tris's trail suddenly vanished? Why had even divination failed to uncover any trace of him? Perhaps their target had undergone a fundamental transformation!

The only question was how he could have become a woman in such a short time--and so effortlessly at that... He even became rather good-looking after the transformation. To be honest, she's quite attractive... Klein's thoughts began to wander.

Leonard nodded with relief. "Yes, that's my theory. It perfectly explains why Instigator Tris seemed to disappear. It also fits the peculiar fact that the upper ranks of the Demoness Sect consist entirely of women."

Dunn and Frye were momentarily speechless.

They had witnessed many monsters and extraordinary phenomena, but this was their first encounter with a transformation of this kind.

"You mean a significant number of the women in the Demoness Sect's upper ranks may once have been men?" Dunn asked. Without waiting for a response, he continued, "That is possible... Perhaps it's a unique characteristic of their potion--of that pathway's potion."

Klein shuddered as he listened. The Demoness Sect's potions sounded like an enormous trap!

Let's hope the Seer pathway doesn't have a similar potion... No, surely it doesn't. That's the Demoness pathway. Even the potion's name sounds ominous. Still, I don't know the name of the Sequence 1 corresponding to Seer...

Klein instinctively began praying to the Goddess.

"Can potions truly accomplish something like that?" Frye asked, still sounding doubtful.

Leonard laughed and spread his hands. "Even low- and mid-Sequence potions can produce unimaginable changes. After all, every one of them originated from the Creator."

Dunn turned toward Klein. "Try divining where the target will appear next."

"All right."

With mixed feelings, Klein walked over to the heap of dresses and selected one, spreading it across the carpet.

He held his cane above the garment, recalled the target's features and all relevant information, then began reciting silently:

"Tris's... No, Trissy's whereabouts.

"Trissy's whereabouts."

After seven repetitions, Klein's pupils darkened from brown to black. A breeze stirred around him.

He released the cane with his left hand, allowing the black shaft to sway.

Despite wobbling unsteadily, it did not fall. It remained upright in its original position.

"There's interference..." Klein said in a grave voice.

Interference means our theory is correct!

That woman was very likely Instigator Tris--no, Trissy!

At this, Dunn gave an almost imperceptible nod.

"The Demoness Sect has remained active since the previous Epoch. It certainly lives up to its reputation..."

Since Tris had transformed into Trissy, Dunn now concluded that she belonged not to the Theosophy Order, but to the Demoness Sect itself.

He surveyed the room and sighed. "We can investigate through other avenues, such as tracing where these clothes were purchased or identifying the owner of this house. We can also have the police patrol the train stations and docks."

We might uncover a few clues that way, but Trissy will certainly have enough time to leave Tingen. Yes... I'll try again above the gray fog once I get home.

The thought of someone like Trissy, who was willing to unleash a massacre on a whim, deeply alarmed Klein. He desperately wanted to find her and execute her on the spot.

"Leonard, go to the police department and bring back a team to finish things here. Klein, you can head home and rest..."

Dunn rubbed his temples and paused for a few seconds. Then, partly to test Klein and partly to instruct him, he asked, "How would you have handled tonight's mission? Assume Leonard, Frye, and I were the only members of your team."

Klein frowned and considered the question for more than ten seconds.

"First, I would use divination to determine whether the ritual was about to take effect. If the answer was no, I would keep watch from a distance and avoid approaching. Then I would ask the police department to deploy officers around the area and assemble at least five cannons. We could bombard the entire building until Trissy's hiding place had been leveled.

"She would either be blown to pieces inside or forced to flee through the cannon fire, exposing herself in the process. I would station the three of you at separate positions to intercept her..."

Klein grew increasingly animated as he spoke. His plan seemed simple and effective, brutal and decisive. Most importantly, it was safe and practical!

Dunn, Leonard, and Frye stared at him in astonishment. For a long moment, no one spoke.

"Captain, is that not a good plan?" Klein's excitement gave way to unease at their silence, and his heart began to pound.

After several seconds, Dunn replied, "No, it is a good plan. But it depends on first confirming that the violent destruction of the altar wouldn't cause an even greater disaster... Sigh. As veteran Nighthawks, we've grown accustomed to relying on ourselves, our Beyonder powers, and our firearms in every situation. We aren't used to involving ordinary people in supernatural incidents..."

Well, I've always been an ardent believer in overwhelming firepower... Klein added inwardly.

Klein and Leonard walked roughly five hundred meters before reaching the carriage stop.

After waiting for a while, they rode back to Iron Cross Street. Leonard headed to the nearby police station, while Klein continued on to Daffodil Street.

Upon reaching his front door, Klein straightened his clothes and made certain everything looked presentable. Then he took out his keys and opened the door.

Melissa and Benson were in the living room beneath the gas lamp, one quietly working on her assignments while the other read a book.

Benson must be exhausted after working all day, yet he still forces himself to study after coming home. What determination... I couldn't manage that. All I can think about is lying down...

Klein glanced at his brother with a smile and raised a hand in silent greeting.

Benson smiled back. "I now understand the price of a handsome salary."

"Everything in this world has a price. We must give something before we can receive anything in return," Klein replied as he placed his cane in the rack beside the door.

"Wasn't that something Emperor Roselle said?" Melissa stopped writing and looked up.

Unlike universities and public schools, Tingen Technical School allowed only two weeks of summer vacation, from late July to early August. Classes resumed as soon as the hottest days had passed.

"Was it? I don't remember..." Klein answered, his expression growing slightly stiff.

He removed his hat and started upstairs, intending to divine Trissy's whereabouts as soon as possible.

Just then, his stomach growled, and a powerful wave of hunger struck him.

Oh, right. I haven't eaten dinner. But the note I left said the security company would provide food, so I told them not to save any for me... Honestly, Captain, how could you forget something like that?

Klein's expression shifted several times as he prepared to pretend he was already full.

At that moment, Melissa turned toward him and pointed to the kitchen.

"We saved you a small lamb chop and a bowl of thick vegetable soup. There are a few slices of bread left as well."

With that, she lowered her head to her work again and muttered, "I thought the meals they provided at work probably wouldn't be very good. You might have lost your appetite..."

Chapter 126: Divination Isn't Omnipotent

Sis, you worry too much--no, you're simply thorough!

Klein immediately perked up. Smiling, he said, "Your concern is entirely justified, Melissa. I actually am a little hungry. Let me change and take a shower first."

Although his mouth was already watering, confirming Instigator Trissy's whereabouts was far more important.

No one knew what insane measures that bastard might take to exact revenge on society!

"All right." Melissa did not look up, continuing to review her schoolwork.

Tap! Tap! Tap!

Klein hurried upstairs and entered his bedroom.

He locked the door, removed his jacket and shoulder holster, then took a simple silver dagger from a drawer.

After sealing the room with a spiritual wall, he drew a breath, calmed himself, and took four steps counterclockwise.

Following the familiar incantation, Klein once again appeared in the majestic palace above the gray fog. He was gradually growing accustomed to the deranged ravings that accompanied the journey.

Having already performed several rituals that day, he felt somewhat fatigued and massaged his temples. With a thought, he conjured a sheet of brown goatskin parchment atop the long bronze table.

After careful consideration, Klein wrote the divination statement:

"Trissy's whereabouts."

He was not certain whether he had spelled the name correctly, but he could supplement it with her appearance and other detailed information.

Holding the parchment, Klein leaned back in his chair. He recalled everything related to Trissy, then recited the statement seven times.

He emptied his mind, closed his eyes, and entered a dream with the aid of Cogitation.

Within the illusory, fog-shrouded scene, he saw a steam locomotive belching thick smoke and sparks. Beyond it were rows of leather seats inside a clean railway carriage.

The gentle, sweet-looking Trissy sat beside a window, her face round and her eyes long and narrow. A checkered hat with a mesh veil rested on the table before her.

Klein repeatedly tried to identify the train's number, but could make nothing out.

Before long, the strain became unbearable, forcing him out of the dream. The long bronze table and the illusory crimson stars reappeared before him.

"All I could confirm was that Trissy took a steam train out of Tingen. There weren't any further clues... Sigh. It seems this mysterious space can help eliminate interference, but it doesn't do much to improve the quality of my divinations..."

Klein tapped the edge of the table while considering his next move.

The divination had allowed him to confirm beyond doubt that their target had once been Instigator Tris. But the newly transformed Trissy was already fleeing Tingen, so Klein doubted this latest result would be of any use to Dunn.

He quickly reached a decision.

The Captain already said he would send telegrams to Backlund, Enmat Harbor, and the other major stops along the railway, placing Trissy on the nationwide wanted list. I won't report this divination. It might only draw suspicion toward me...

Regardless of whether Klein warned him, Dunn had already adopted the most appropriate follow-up measures.

Since he had failed to see the train number in the dream, using spirit pendulum divination or other methods would be equally futile, even if he tried to narrow it down through elimination.

It was just like the situation with the red chimney.

Feeling mentally exhausted, Klein did not remain above the gray fog. He enveloped himself in spirituality and simulated the sensation of falling.

The moment he returned to his room, his thoughts filled with succulent, glistening lamb.

"I have to add some fennel... Praise the Lady!"

Klein swallowed, swiftly removed the spiritual wall, and opened the door.

At twenty minutes to nine the following morning, he entered the Blackthorn Security Company with his cane in hand.

"Good morning, Klein! I have wonderful news!" Rozanne called excitedly from behind the reception desk, waving her hands.

Klein's eyes brightened. "We caught Trissy?"

"Trissy? Who's that?" Rozanne, dressed in green, stared at him blankly.

"...You probably wouldn't know her. What's the good news?" Klein smoothly changed the subject.

Rozanne beamed. "The Captain's request has been approved! The police department is transferring two officers who have encountered supernatural incidents to serve as clerks here. I finally won't have to stay up all night so often! Praise the Lady!"

"That is wonderful news," Klein agreed sincerely.

After exchanging a few more pleasantries with Rozanne, he passed through the partition and headed underground, intending to continue his mysticism lessons.

As he passed the Captain's office and the Nighthawks' recreation room, Klein poked his head inside. Dunn, Leonard, and the others were still present, which meant the previous night's search and subsequent investigation had yielded nothing useful. The tedious follow-up work would now be left to the police department.

Klein initially wanted to speak with the Captain and ask for an update, but Dunn was busy composing telegrams, so he decided not to disturb him. He could ask again at lunch.

Following the stairs underground, Klein came upon the two classical gas lamps mounted in metal frames. Their light shone through glass panes, illuminating the perpetually silent corridor.

He inhaled the cool, refreshing air and took several steps before abruptly stopping.

His head snapped toward one of the gas lamps, his brow gradually furrowing.

He had made a crucial mistake!

It was a mistake only someone possessing knowledge from Earth would make.

In his divination above the gray fog the previous night, Klein had seen Trissy aboard a steam train. He had therefore assumed without thinking that the scene was occurring at that very moment.

But this world had not yet invented electric lighting or anything similar. Once darkness fell, almost no passenger trains remained in operation. Accustomed to trains that ran throughout the night, Klein had instinctively overlooked that fact!

In other words, the scene had not taken place the previous night.

It was a glimpse of the future!

That meant it would happen today or tomorrow!

Klein's nerves tightened. After pacing back and forth, he hurried upstairs again.

He knocked on the recreation-room door and entered. Leonard stood by the window, helplessly reciting a poem.

Ignoring Kenley, Royale, and Seeka Tron, who were playing cards, Klein looked at Leonard.

"I need to ask you something."

"Do you want to learn a few tricks for entertaining the ladies?" Leonard teased, setting down his copy of Selected Poems by Roselle.

He left the recreation room and followed Klein halfway down the stairs leading underground. Looking into Klein's eyes, he chuckled.

"It seems your divination last night was successful."

Klein offered no explanation and went straight to the point. "I divined that Trissy would leave aboard a steam train."

After their conversation at the workhouse in the West Borough, Klein no longer minded appearing somewhat unusual in front of Leonard.

"A steam train... The earliest one departs at seven in the morning." Leonard pulled out his pocket watch, flipped it open, and glanced at the time. "There's no time to lose. I'll tell the Captain I received information from a reliable source."

He rushed upstairs and left the Blackthorn Security Company. After waiting outside for a few minutes, he returned and entered Dunn Smith's office.

Klein sighed in relief. He watched as the Captain summoned the other Nighthawks from their card game and sent a telegram. Before long, they hurried out the door.

Reflecting on what had just happened, Klein felt conflicted. This lesson differed from the one he had learned through the suited clown's death, yet the mistake shared similar qualities. That similarity seemed to deepen his understanding and left a stronger impression on him.

He passed the armory and entered the duty room, casually removing his top hat and coat and hanging them on the rack.

Old Neil had just finished brewing a cup of freshly ground coffee. He took a contented sip and asked, "Would you like some?"

"Certainly." Klein sat down, as relaxed as if he had returned home.

Old Neil glanced at him and frowned in mock reproach. "Still three sugar cubes and a spoonful of milk? You have quite a sweet tooth. That's bad for your teeth and your health."

"No, no. I only prefer sweetness in my coffee. With grilled steak or roast meat, I like rose salt, black pepper, fennel, and other seasonings." Klein had always considered himself a lover of every flavor.

Old Neil quickly prepared the coffee and pushed it across the table.

"Would you like to rest, or shall we begin immediately?"

"Give me a few minutes to settle down. The Captain and the others received information about Trissy's whereabouts and are on their way to the railway station. I wonder how things will turn out..." Klein sighed.

Old Neil clicked his tongue. "Was the information detailed enough? Do they know which train?"

"No, that wasn't confirmed," Klein replied, pursing his lips.

Old Neil suddenly laughed. "Under those circumstances, failure is far more likely than success. Trissy should be a Sequence 7 Beyonder, and someone at that level won't be caught so easily. Heh heh. Never depend too heavily on divination. Divination isn't omnipotent. It provides only symbolic revelations, which can easily be misinterpreted or cause you to overlook something."

Recalling his mistake, Klein felt a surge of melancholy. He nodded earnestly.

"Yes. Divination isn't omnipotent."

As the words left his mouth, he sighed. His body, mind, and soul suddenly entered a wondrous state. He leaned back slightly, intending to exhale, when an illusory sound of shattering rang in his ears.

Something within him seemed to dissolve and merge completely with his spirit.

Klein half closed his eyes and silently experienced the strange, indescribable sensation.

No one needed to tell him what had happened.

He had completely digested the Seer potion.

The first town the Tussock River reached after leaving Tingen was called Wienia. It was also the first railway stop between Tingen and Backlund.

On the platform, Trissy had changed into a long beige dress and a round women's hat. A fine mesh veil hung from its brim, covering half her face and rendering her features indistinct.

She had already sent a telegram to her associate in Tingen, warning them to be careful. In it, she claimed that she had used stolen money to purchase a train ticket to Backlund.

Trissy had chosen not to board in Tingen, instead traveling downstream to Wienia, because she still possessed the instincts and extensive experience of an Assassin.

Woo!

With a long, piercing whistle, the great metal beast pulled alongside the platform, belching smoke and sparks as it came to a halt.

Carrying no luggage, Trissy boarded the first carriage. At the same time, she resolved to disembark after three stops and reach Backlund by some other means.

In the basement of Saint Selena Cathedral, Klein sat with his eyes closed, leaning back in his chair.

As he experienced the potion's complete digestion, he dimly perceived one illusory star after another. Each seemed to share an inexplicable connection with him, as though they longed to gather and fuse into one.

Once the indescribable hunger and thirst subsided, Klein returned to normal. No further sensations followed.

But my mind feels much lighter and purer...

He opened his eyes.

At that moment, he knew he had become a true and complete Seer.

Chapter 127: Laying the Groundwork

Light from the gas lamp filtered through its glass shade and illuminated the duty room. Old Neil finished leafing through the newspaper, took a sip of coffee, and looked at Klein.

"How do you feel now? Have you calmed down, or do you need a glass of wine, an advance on your salary, or perhaps a day off?"

Having completely digested the Seer potion, Klein was using Cogitation to change the "switch" that activated his Spirit Vision. He wanted something less conspicuous.

He no longer needed a physical gesture to activate Spirit Vision, which meant he could adopt a subtler trigger--perhaps rapidly rubbing the joint of his middle finger with his thumb, or clicking his left molars twice.

Klein considered situations in which he might need Spirit Vision while holding a revolver in one hand and a cane in the other. In the end, he chose the latter method: two clicks of his left molars to activate Spirit Vision, and two clicks of his right molars to deactivate it.

After repeatedly reinforcing the mental suggestion, he completed the change. He opened his eyes and smiled.

"I was only worried about the Captain's operation. I don't need anything to calm me down."

At the same time, he clicked his left molars twice and activated Spirit Vision, eager to familiarize himself with the new method as quickly as possible.

"Cough! Cough! Cough!"

Old Neil suddenly began coughing so violently that his face turned as red as a cooked lobster.

"What happened?" Klein asked, momentarily startled and genuinely concerned.

He carefully examined Old Neil's aura, but the colors representing his health appeared normal, merely somewhat dimmed by age.

After coughing for nearly twenty seconds, Old Neil finally found relief. He reached for his coffee and slowly took a sip.

"Everyone makes mistakes. Ahem. I choked on my drink just now... Shall we begin today's mysticism lesson?"

"All right."

Klein silently clicked his right molars twice.

He was both delighted and frustrated that he had fully digested the Seer potion one or two weeks earlier than expected. Naturally, he was thrilled to be free of the danger of losing control and to have the opportunity to advance soon, gaining even more Beyonder powers. Anyone would have been excited.

Yet the development had also disrupted all his plans and schedules.

Since he would need to remain with the Tingen Nighthawks for some time, Klein did not believe secretly advancing to Clown would be wise. He would constantly fear discovery and be unable to use his new abilities during missions, placing himself in even greater danger.

Instead, he intended to follow Spirit Medium Daly's example and submit an application to the higher-ups. He would use his accumulated contributions to obtain the formula and extraordinary ingredients, then officially advance as a Sequence 8 Nighthawk.

However, mastering a potion in one month was vastly different from mastering it in a year. Klein could withstand an investigation by the Holy Cathedral and be classified as a promising talent worthy of cultivation, but he did not want the higher-ups to become suspicious of him. He needed a convincing explanation for his progress.

He had originally planned to spend the period before completely digesting the Seer potion laying the groundwork with the Captain. For example, he could mention that his spirituality felt increasingly active whenever he visited the Divination Club. He could casually describe the principles of being a Seer that he had discovered while telling fortunes for others. He could also report that he no longer heard voices he should not hear or saw things he should not see.

In this way, the Nighthawks' higher-ups might conclude that, while carrying out his "assignment," he had inadvertently stumbled upon the same discovery Daly had made--only more thoroughly.

Their attention would then be focused on summarizing the underlying principles and uncovering the acting method, rather than on suspecting Klein.

I might even be able to help the Captain and the others learn about the acting method...

Klein believed Dunn Smith was a good captain. Aside from his poor memory, he had no glaring faults. Klein therefore hoped to reduce Dunn's risk of losing control and help him grow stronger.

Of course, Klein could simply wait a year before applying, thereby avoiding most of the risk. But the succession of suspicious coincidences and the red chimney he had seen through dream divination left him no choice but to improve his abilities as quickly as possible.

I'll lay the groundwork with the Captain three or four times over the next two weeks, then formally submit my application. In the meantime, I can visit the underground market and see whether any of the necessary extraordinary ingredients are available. They'll probably be very expensive...

Having made his decision, Klein returned his attention to the mysticism lesson.

Time passed swiftly, and lunchtime soon approached. Old Neil finished his coffee and cleared the materials from the table with a laugh.
